› Message from fabrik:builder
  I built the engine's differential tests for several statements in one `Exec`;
  every check in the brief passes and no fault in s4ql turned up. The branch is
  `several-statements-engine` and the last commit is `43439328`. There are four
  commits on top of `main`, and nothing is pushed.

  **What the tests now judge.** `TestDifferential` and `TestConn_differential`
  run the profile `several` and the several-statement texts of `mixed` through
  `ExecAll`. This is `pair.several` in `/internal/engine/several_test.go`.
  - **The walk.** The instance runs each statement alone through the existing
  `pair.step`, so every rule for a statement alone judges it against SQLite.
  Each statement takes as many arguments as it has parameters, and the walk
  stops at the first one that fails. The other instance then runs the whole text
  in one `ExecAll`.
  - **What must match the walk:** the error (kind, message, offset in the whole
  text), the result, the tables (also after a failed call, read inside the
  transaction), and the new commits with their annotations. Inside a transaction
  no commit is allowed, and `TestConn_differential` compares both instances'
  whole logs after each transaction ends.
  - **The oracle's own split.** It reads statements with SQLite's own tokenizer
  plus `sqlite3_complete`, not with `stmt.Split`. The refusal of spaces has its
  own rule on SQLite's tokens (`spaceRule`), and "more arguments than
  parameters" has its own message rule.
  - **Evaluation errors from another row.** The two instances draw scan
  directions from different seeds, so `ExecAll` can fail on another row than the
  walk did. That error must belong to the statement where the walk stopped and
  is justified by `pair.justify`.

  **Reach kinds.** Floors are at a third of the 2026-11-26 totals: 28 kinds in
  `TestDifferential.kinds`, 35 in `TestConn_differential.kinds`. Totals are
  given as TestDifferential / TestConn_differential:

  | Kind | Totals |
  |---|---|
  | Ran to the end, 2/3/4/5 statements | 129/92, 133/121, 93/62, 55/40 |
  | Stopped at a constraint (first/middle/last) | 137/84, 89/56, 37/25 |
  | Stopped at an evaluation error (first/middle/last) | 13/27, 10/14, 3/1 |
  | Stopped at not supported (first/middle/last) | 238/226, 86/96, 46/31 |
  | Stopped at SQLite's error (first/middle/last) | 339/713, 123/153, 63/72 |
  | Stopped at too few arguments (first/middle/last) | 16/19, 8/7, 7/8 |
  | Fewer / more arguments than parameters | 31/34, 129/134 |
  | Text in a write / read-only transaction | 868 / 557 |
  | Failed text, then commit / rollback (write tx) | 161 / 81 |
  | Failed text, then commit / rollback (read-only tx) | 114 / 59 |
  | One commit / two or more commits, outside a transaction | 317/110, 392/125 |
  | Statement on a table created / changed earlier in the text | 475/385,
  329/227 |
  | Last statement with RETURNING ran | 204/162 |

  The stop at the last statement on an evaluation error has a floor only in
  `TestDifferential`, since `TestConn_differential` reaches it once.

  **Generator fix.** `severalReturning` ends some texts in a create table, an
  update of every row, and an insert with RETURNING. The driver check's
  RETURNING excuse is reached 44 times again and is pinned exactly in the
  `.tsv`. A floor line for it in `.kinds` was refused because the kind is exact.
  I raised the floor of "last statement with RETURNING that ran" to 40. All
  affected `.tsv` files and the `mixed` and `several` digests are recounted.

  **Planted faults: all 7 caught, each reverted.**
  - All statements in one commit: caught by the commits and annotations check.
  - A failed statement's commit written anyway: caught by the tables check.
  - Annotation holds the whole text: caught by the annotations check.
  - The statement after a failed one runs: caught by the tables check.
  - Second statement's arguments one too late: caught by the error check.
  - A select skipped: caught by the error check.
  - A failure inside a transaction undoes the statements before it: caught by
  the tables read inside the transaction. This plant rolls back and re-begins
  the whole transaction, because the core keeps only one savepoint at a time.

  **Checks run:** `make lint` and `make test` pass. The whole engine package
  (380 s) and the driver check pass without `-short`. The new tests pass with
  `-race -count=20`, and both differential tests pass under `-race`.

  **Open:**
  - Self-review fixed two real issues: a table only the other instance had
  inside a transaction went unread, and a leftover subquery observer could force
  values into `ExecAll`.
  - Two minor review findings are recorded, not fixed. The transaction
  bookkeeping in `txDifferential` counts nothing of a several-statement text,
  which only lowers some reach counts. A failure that depends on the other
  instance's random seed replays with the `-directions` seed the failing test
  logs, not with the sequence seed alone.
  - `gofmt -l` flags `/internal/engine/differential_test.go` because of one long
  line I did not change. I left it alone.
  - The scratch directory `/Volumes/990/s4ql/several-statements-engine/` stays
  until the branch is merged.

  Docs are in `/docs/todo/several-statements.md` (section "The engine's
  differential tests") and the diary in

› Message from general-purpose
  This is a fault of the test, not of `internal/store/s3store`. The real bucket
  answered five gets with an error, those five racers stopped early, and the
  test still expected all 32 increments. The log shows no lost increment, though
  the test as written could not prove there was none either.

  1. **Which S3:** the real Tigris bucket, not a fake. `TestClient_conformance`
  (`internal/store/s3store/s3store_test.go:980`) uses `realBucket`. `.env.test`
  sets `S4QL_S3_BUCKET=sql4s3` and `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_S3=https://t3.storage.dev`,
  and the job has the AWS secrets in its environment (job log lines 332-333).
  **Where the 408 comes from:** Tigris sent it. The log reads "https response
  error StatusCode: 408 … RequestCanceled" (job log lines 366-370), and the
  RequestIDs are Tigris's numeric ones. It is not the SDK: `s3store.go:194-195`
  sets `NopRetryer` and zero attempts, and the transport has no timeout
  (`s3store.go:147-160`). It is not our context either: the context is
  `t.Context()`, and an ended context would have made `ended(ctx, err)`
  (`s3store.go:270`) wrap `context.Canceled`, which the message would show.
  2. **Fault of the test.** At `storetest.go:557`, `read` reports the error with
  `t.Errorf` and the racer returns with fewer than 4 increments done. The final
  check at line 582 still wants `racers*increments` = 32, and its message
  "after 32 increments" names the expected count, not the count done. The
  numbers fit: the counter's text "21 racer-0-3" means racer 0 finished. If 3
  racers finished, they made 12 increments, so the 5 that stopped made 9 between
  them, and their unfinished increments are the missing 11 (that range is 5 to
  20). The test never counts the swaps that succeeded, so it can neither prove
  nor rule out a lost increment. The fix adds that count. The s3store code
  swallows nothing here: the Get error is returned as it came.
  3. **History:** it failed the same way once before, in run 45375324120 on
  `main` (2026-11-24): "racer 4: swap … outcome unknown … read: connection reset
  by peer", and the counter was "29 racer-7-3". So that is 2 of the last 40
  failed CI runs, both a transient error from Tigris during this race of 8
  racers. I checked the logs of the 10 most recent failed runs and found no
  other occurrence.
  4. **Reproduction:** not possible locally, because it needs the real bucket
  and its credentials, so I ran nothing against the bucket.
  5. **Proposed fix** (test only, not committed): the diff is at
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9e
  b0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/flake/fix.diff` (108 lines,
  `internal/store/storetest/storetest.go`, `raceCounter`):
     - A new helper, `get`, works like `read` but returns the error instead of
  reporting it.
     - Each racer tries again after a failed get, or after a swap whose outcome
  is unknown (an error other than ErrChanged and the not-applied errors). Once
  one racer has failed 5 times it reports with the last error, so a store that
  keeps failing still fails the test.
     - The test counts, under a mutex, the swaps that succeeded and those whose
  outcome is unknown, and the last read is tried again too.
     - The final check is `applied <= count <= applied+unknown`, in place of
  `count == 32`.
     - The answers NotFound, Exists, Checksum and TooLarge to a swap still fail
  at once.

     **What it must not hide:** a lost increment leaves the counter below
  `applied` and still fails, and a double-applied swap gives more than
  `applied+unknown` and still fails. It is a count of events, not a time bound,
  so it holds on any machine and under any load. I checked it locally with the
  fake, which is not a test of the real bucket: `go vet` is clean, and under
  `-race` storetest and simstore passed with `-count=5` and
  `TestClient_conformanceOnFake` with `-count=10`. Before merging, a planted
  swap that ignores If-Match on the fake should be run, to show the check still
  catches a lost increment.

  The worktree is clean: branch `s3store-flake` is at `origin/main` with no
  changes.

  bucket (Tigris) answered five gets with HTTP 408 during the race of 8 clients,
  those racers stopped early, and the test still expected all 32 increments.
  The log shows no lost increment. It has happened twice in the last 40 failed

› Message from general-purpose
  I found no fault in s4ql. Of 14 faults I planted, 12 were caught and 2
  survived. One survivor is a small gap in the tests, the other changes nothing
  the engine shows. Worktree is clean at 43439328 on branch
  `several-statements-engine-review`. My scratch directory on /Volumes/990 is
  deleted.

  **Findings, most important first (all are test gaps, not s4ql faults):**
  1. **Nothing checks how many times a statement runs, if a second run changes
  nothing.** Planted in `internal/engine/several.go` `execPart`: in a read-only
  transaction, each select of a text runs twice. It survived. Only events and
  `transaction.statements` would show it, and the differential tests compare
  neither. The same holds for any rerun of a statement that has no effect, such
  as a select or a `create table if not exists`. A `TestConn_ExecAll` subtest
  that counts the events of one text in a transaction would close it.
  2. **The RETURNING excuse in the driver check does not check mattn's number.**
  `severalExcuse`
  (`internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck/drivercheck_test.go:5480`) excuses
  any mismatch when the last statement has RETURNING. It never requires mattn to
  give the count of the statement before. The excuse for an empty statement at
  the end does require mattn's 0. s4ql's own count is still judged against
  `changes()` (line 5460). This is older than the branch and minor.
  3. **A stop at the last statement on an evaluation error:** `TestDifferential`
  reaches it 3 times (floor 1) and `TestConn_differential` once (no row). The
  first and middle positions have floors in both tests, so I don't think closing
  it is worth much.
  4. **The `gofmt -l` report predates the branch.** The unformatted line is 1155
  (the `checkArithmetic` closure), and `main`'s copy of `differential_test.go`
  gives the same report.

  **Q1, soundness.** I think the comparison is not circular:
  - The oracle reads the statements with SQLite's tokenizer and
  `sqlite3_complete`, not `stmt.Split`.
  - It writes the refusal of spaces and the "too many arguments" message from
  its own rules.
  - Each statement of the walk is judged against SQLite by `pair.step`.
  - What both sides share is `stmt.Check` and `Parameters()`, by design. Each
  statement alone is judged against SQLite on that.
  - The excuse for an evaluation error on another row cannot hide a stop at the
  wrong statement. It requires the error's offset to lie inside the statement
  where the walk stopped (`several_test.go:707`), and then `pair.justify` must
  accept it. Without LIMIT and subqueries, the scan direction decides only which
  row fails, not whether one does.
  - Small weakening: `Supported: c.Each` is false for a whole text when any one
  of its statements is unsupported, so a wrong refusal by `stmt.Check` inside
  such a text is only judged as "SQLite prepares it".

  **Q2, planted faults (one at a time, each reverted):**

  | # | Fault | Result | What caught it |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 1 | Last statement skipped when the text has no final `;` | caught | judging
  check, the result |
  | 2 | Offset of a later refused statement counted from its part | caught |
  judging check, the error |
  | 3 | Result is the first statement's | caught | judging check, the result |
  | 4 | After a "not supported" error in the middle, the next statements still
  run | caught | error, result and tables |
  | 5 | In a read-only transaction, a select runs twice | **survived** | see
  finding 1 |
  | 6 | Too many arguments: the statements run first and are refused afterwards
  | caught | error and result |
  | 7 | A comment at the end reruns the last statement | caught | error |
  | 8 | In a transaction, the last statement does not run but logs success |
  caught | result |
  | 9 | `split.go`: the text is not trimmed at its end | survived (no effect) |
  `TestSplit` catches it; in the engine, annotations and tables are identical,
  which I checked with a temporary test |
  | 10 | `split.go` `trimmedEnd`: `Offset: pos` instead of `max(pos, end)` |
  caught | judging check, the error |
  | 11 | The result of a failed statement is not counted | caught | judging
  check, the result |
  | 12 | `before()` refuses the whole text up front for a later refused
  statement | caught | judging check, the error |
  | 13 | `engine.go` `write` plus `several.go`: the second statement is
  annotated with the first statement's arguments | caught | the annotations
  check |
  | 14 | `tx.go` `writeIn`: in a transaction, the last statement's annotation is
  wrong | caught | `pair.sameLog` after the transaction
  (`TestConn_differential` only) |

  Every catch except number 9 was a judging check; no catch came from a count
  alone.

  **Q3, reach counts.** I ran both tests three times with `S4QL_RECOUNT=1`. All
  three `.tsv` files came out byte-identical to the committed ones, so no
  required count varies. The only count that varies, the justified evaluation
  error on another row, is not required.

  **Q4, generator.** `TestNewSequence` passes, so only the digests of `mixed`
  and `several` changed. In the driver's `.tsv` only `mixed` and `several` rows
  changed. In the engine's `.tsv` files only `mixed`, `several`, and the
  simulation's runs changed. `TestGenerated` passed, and the RETURNING excuse
  was reached 34 times in `several` and 10 in `mixed`, the pinned 44. It is
  judged only loosely (finding 2).

  **Q5, the builder's two open findings.** Neither matters much. The
  `txDifferential` bookkeeping only lowers counts of other kinds; it judges
  nothing. The `-directions` seed affects only how a failure is replayed, and
  the counts are deterministic.

  **Q6, code review.** No bugs found. No test skips itself or depends on timing;
  the cancel test cancels from its logger. The comments match the code.

  **Not checked:** `-race`, `make test` and the lint target, `TestSimulation`,
  and faults in `internal/core` that would hit a statement alone as well, which
  the walk shares by design and only the single-statement checks against SQLite
  can catch.

  29 checks were in pass or skipping, and none needed a rerun.

  1. **How many times a statement runs.** `pair.several`
  (`internal/engine/several_test.go`) now records the statement events of the
  walk and of `ExecAll` and compares their number, order, kind, outcome and
  transaction kind. In a transaction it also compares the transaction's count of
  statements, through a new `Conn.TxStatements` in `tx_export_test.go`. A text
  refused before any statement runs must log exactly one event, with no kind.
  Proof: I planted "in a read-only transaction each select runs twice" in
  `execPart`, and `TestConn_differential` failed on the events (three logged
  where the walk logged two). An extra count in the transaction alone fails it
  on the count check. Both plants were reverted.
  2. **The RETURNING excuse.** I ran mattn to find its rule: its result is
  SQLite's count of changes just before the last statement. That count comes
  from the last statement before it that sets one, or from an earlier call if
  none does. I also checked that savepoint, rollback-to and release keep
  SQLite's count. The excuse now requires exactly that number, which the new
  `severalBeforeLast` measures; a new `TestSeveral` case pins the earlier-call
  case. Proof: planting "mattn's number plus one" fails `TestGenerated` in 1.4
  s, where the old excuse accepted any number.
  3. **Support per statement.** `stmttest.Call` has a new field `Supports`, one
  bool per joined statement, and `Each` is now their AND; the generator draws
  the same as before. The engine's walk passes each statement its own value. If
  the walk ever reaches more parts than the generator wrote, the test fails
  rather than guessing. I planted no fault for this one, since the brief asked
  for proofs of 1 and 2 only.

  - **No fault in s4ql** turned up, and no `.kinds` or `.tsv` count changed.
  - **Docs:** the section "Fixes after the review of the engine's differential
  tests" in `docs/todo/several-statements.md`, the roadmap lines (driver area
  and the "At a glance" table), and step 3 of the diary. The only conflict when
  merging main was in the todo file.
  - **Checks:** `make test` and `make lint` passed. The whole engine package and
  the driver check passed without `-short`. `TestSeveral` and
  `TestSequence_several` passed with `-race -count=20`; the engine's two
  differential tests and `TestConn_ExecAll` ran only 3 times under `-race
  -short`, not 20.
  - **Self-review:** the low-effort code-review skill returned no findings.
  - **Open:** the driver check has the same whole-text problem as fix 3: it
  still passes `Each` for the whole text to each statement of its walk. That was
  outside the brief. The simulation's planted bugs and mutation testing are
  still to come.
  - My scratch directory is deleted. The worktree and the branch are left for
  you to clean up.

          -last nightly runs at the start of a session and before a large
          -r round of sub-agents, with `gh run list --workflow nightly.ym
          -l --limit 3`. A failure goes the same day to a sub-agent that 
          -reproduces every failing seed or input at the run's commit and
          - at `main`, and says whether it is a fault in s4ql. A list of

          -d bug or a recorded difference is shown to cause exactly that 
          -symptom. The workflow never closes its issue itself, so the le
          -ad closes it when a nightly has passed again.

  PR #166 is merged (29 checks pass or skipping; state MERGED); none of the ten
  failures of `TestDifferential -sequences 400` was a fault in s4ql.
  - The ten: 22752, 4558, 7214, 7438, 10030, 10510, 11790 and 13294 failed with
  "the walk reaches its statement N of N, and the generator wrote N-1 texts".
  14222 failed with "statement 3 of 4 alone: insert … (1e999): the engine does
  not support a statement that the pass supports". 8782 gave UNIQUE where SQLite
  gave "string or blob too big".
  - Nine have one cause: the generator miscounted. Every one of those texts
  holds the `Others` form "select * from X; select * from X". SQLite,
  `sqliteParts` and `stmt.Split` all read it as two statements, but
  `Sequence.several` gave it one entry of `Supports`. That shifted every later
  entry (in 14222 the unsupported 1e999 insert took `true`). Fix: one entry per
  statement (`statementsIn`), and the field's docs now say so. No new random
  draws, so no sequence and no count changed.
  - 8782 is the recorded RETURNING difference in a new shape. In sqlite3 with
  `.limit length 1000`, a two-row `update d set k = -0.0, p = x'<995 B>'
  returning p, p` gives "too big", the same update without RETURNING gives
  "UNIQUE constraint failed: d.k", and s4ql gives the UNIQUE.
  `pair.returningTooBig` now accepts that shape only when the error is not a
  length error and equals SQLite's without the limit. It is pinned in
  `TestPair_returning`, which is red on main's tests.
  - Planted faults, each reverted: skipping a write after two selects in
  `ExecAll` is caught by 7438, 10030 and 10510. A wrong UNIQUE message for table

⏺ There is one decision waiting for you.

  Background. A commit in the log holds the rows a statement changed; that is
  the truth, and replay never runs SQL. Beside the rows, a commit holds an
  annotation: the statement's text and its parameters. Nothing depends on
  annotations in production. But the engine's simulation uses them as a check:
  it runs every annotated statement again in SQLite and compares the result with
  the replayed rows.

  The problem. The next feature toward goqite is default (<expression>) with
  strftime and randomblob (goqite's id and timestamps). A statement that reads
  the clock or draws random bytes cannot be run again to the same result unless
  the annotation says what it read. Today the annotation has no place for that.
  The spec already says annotations hold "non-deterministic inputs, such as the
  time and random seeds", but the format never got those fields.

  The question: when a statement reads the clock or random bytes, what does its 
  annotation record?

  - (a) Nothing. Costs nothing, but such statements lose the re-run check, and
    the spec's sentence would have to go.
  - (b) The time it read, and every run of random bytes it drew, in order. A few
    bytes per statement (16 for a goqite id). The re-run check stays exact. A
    huge randomblob(n) is stored twice, in the row and in the annotation.
  - (c) The time, and one seed from which s4ql derives its random bytes. Small,
    but the generator's algorithm becomes part of the format, anyone who reads
    the log can predict the values, and it bends the agreed rule "every call
    gives new bytes from the host's source".

  I recommend (b). It changes two words in the spec's sentence, "random seeds"
  to "random bytes".

※ recap: Goal: run glue and goqite on s4ql; arithmetic and several statements in one Exec are built, and the last checks for the latter are landing now. Next action is yours: choose what the annotation records for time and randomness, a, b, or c.

❯ Are we sure it's a good idea that we actually run the statements _which are   
  supposed to be informational only_ to run correctness checks?                 

⏺ No, and your question shows I framed the decision badly.

  What the re-run does today. Only the engine's simulation does it, never
  production. After crashes and recoveries it takes the log, runs each commit's
  annotated statement in SQLite in log order, and compares that with the rows
  the log replays to. So the annotations serve the test twice: as the link from
  a commit to the statement that made it, and as something whose fidelity is
  itself checked (a planted bug that writes a wrong annotation is caught this
  way).

  Is that a good idea? In itself I think yes: a test may cross-check
  informational data against the truth, as long as nothing in production depends
  on it, and nothing does. A wrong annotation would mislead whoever reads the
  log, so checking it has value.

  What is not a good idea is letting the test's needs shape the format, and that
  is what I did: I said option (a) "loses the re-run check". That is not a
  reason. The simulation's clock and randomness come from a seeded host, and the
  tests already get a statement's subquery values through a test hook and
  substitute them when they re-run. Time and random bytes can come the same way,
  with nothing added to the annotation. I believe that works; I would have it
  confirmed before building.

  So the decision should be made on what the annotation is for, not on the test:

  - (a) Text and parameters only: "what the client sent". The simplest
    definition, and consistent with informational. The stored random value is in
    the row anyway, and the commit already carries a time.
  - (b) Also the time and random bytes the statement read: worth it only if you
    want the log to answer "what exactly did this statement see", for audit or
    debugging.

  With the test argument gone, I now lean to (a). It means the spec's phrase
  "and their non-deterministic inputs, such as the time and random seeds" comes
  out. Which do you want?

✻ Cogitated for 3h 36m 12s · done 11:09 AM

❯ I actually think it's nice that time and random bytes are part of the         
  annotation. BUT: given that we can have multiple timestamps per query in a    
  transaction, wouldn't that bloat the annotation a lot?                        

⏺ No, time cannot bloat it. The bloat risk is in the random bytes.

  Time. By the rule we agreed, a statement reads the clock once, so 'now' has
  one value throughout it, however many times and rows it is used for. Inside a
  transaction each statement reads it again, and each statement has its own
  annotation anyway. So it is at most one integer (about 8 bytes) per statement,
  and absent when the statement did not read the clock. The statement's text
  beside it is already far larger.

  Random bytes. Here every call draws new bytes, so the size follows the number
  of calls. An insert with goqite's default (... randomblob(16) ...) adds 16
  bytes. But update t set token = randomblob(16) over 10,000 rows would add 160
  KB to the annotation. Those bytes are almost always also stored in the rows of
  the same commit, so in the worst case such a commit about doubles; it cannot
  grow beyond that order.

  If the doubling bothers you, the annotation could record random bytes only up
  to a cap and mark itself as cut off beyond it. Such a statement would then not
  be fully described in the log, and the test would take the rest from its
  hook.

  My suggestion: record the time and all random bytes, with no cap for now. The
  formats may still break freely, so a cap can come later if real commits show
  it is needed.

  Do you agree with that, or do you want the cap from the start?

✻ Churned for 13s · done 11:11 AM

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed: all of it, no cap.

  The spec needs one sentence changed, and its wording is yours to approve.
  Today it says:

  ▎ Next to the results, a commit object stores the SQL statements that produced
  ▎ them, with their parameters and their non-deterministic inputs, such as the
  ▎ time and random seeds.

  I propose:

  ▎ Next to the results, a commit object stores the SQL statements that produced
  ▎ them, with their parameters and their non-deterministic inputs: the time 
  ▎ that a statement read, and all the random bytes that it drew.

  The rest of the bullet stays ("They are annotations, not the truth, and replay
  never depends on them."). Is that wording right?

  So the smallest honest fix is two words, "random seeds" to "random bytes", and
  nothing else. My longer rewording was more than needed. Shall I make that

❯ So do we store the bytes in the commit object now?                            

⏺ Not yet. It is decided, not built.

  Today a commit object's annotation holds only a statement's text and its
  parameters, and no statement can read the clock or draw random bytes yet, so
  there is nothing to record.

  The fields come with the second of the three pieces, when strftime and
  randomblob are built. Then each statement's annotation, which is part of the
  commit object beside the row changes, gets the time it read and the random
  bytes it drew. The row changes stay the truth; replay never reads the
  annotation.

  its statement pass.

✻ Crunched for 25s · done 11:23 AM

❯ Let's say we have a statement "update t1 set updated = strftime(…);". In      
  SQLite, doesn't that give a new time to every row also? (In contrast to       
  Postgres?)                                                                    
  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ Also in a tx                                                                  

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No. I ran it in SQLite 3.54.1 instead of answering from memory:

  - One statement, many rows: update t set u = strftime(..., 'now') over
    3,000,000 rows gave exactly 1 distinct time. A select returning 3,000,000
    rows one by one also gave 1. So within one statement, every row gets the
    same time.
  - In a transaction: two updates in one transaction, with work between them,
    gave two different times (…57.981Z and …58.395Z). So each statement reads
    the clock again.

  That is where SQLite differs from Postgres, whose now() is the transaction's
  start time for every statement in it. It matches the rule we wrote in the
  spec: one value per statement, and each statement of a transaction reads the
  clock again.

  For the annotation this means: at most one time value per statement, never one
  per row. The per-row growth can only come from random bytes.

✻ Churned for 26s · done 11:25 AM

❯ And it's not just because SQLite is really fast and it's millisecond          
  precision?                                                                    

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ Or is the distinction one time per tx in Postgres vs one per statement in     
  SQLite?                                                                       

⏺ Stop Task
  ⎿  sqlite3 :memory: "create table t(a integer primary key, u text); with
     recursive c(x) as (select 1 union all select x+1 from c where x <
     3000000) insert into t(a… · stopped

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It is not speed or precision. I measured it this time:

  - One update over 400,000 rows ran for 744 ms by the clock (10:27:45.847
    before, 10:27:46.591 after).
  - All 400,000 rows got the same time, 10:27:45.847, the instant the statement
    started.

  With millisecond precision and 744 ms of running, a per-row clock would have
  given hundreds of distinct values. SQLite's documentation says the same: 'now'
  is fixed for the duration of one statement.

  And yes, your second message states the distinction exactly: one time per 
  transaction in Postgres (now() is the transaction's start), one time per 
  statement in SQLite. Inside a SQLite transaction, two statements get two
  times, as the earlier test showed. Our spec rule follows SQLite.

  One confession: my first attempt at this measurement was badly sized
  (3,000,000 rows with 2,000 random bytes each) and ate about 10 GB of memory
  and swap before I stopped it. Swap is draining again, and nothing else was
  affected.

✻ Worked for 2m 24s · done 11:27 AM

❯ No worries. Okay, but one statement with randomblob gives a new random blob   
  to each row affected, right?                                                  

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Right. Tested: update t set r = randomblob(16) over 100,000 rows gave 100,000
  distinct blobs. Every row gets its own bytes, because the function is called
  once per row.

  So the two behave differently within one statement: the time is read once,
  random bytes are drawn per call. That is why "all" for the annotation means
  one time value but as many runs of random bytes as there were calls: for that
  update, 100,000 × 16 bytes, beside the same bytes in the rows.

✻ Brewed for 6s · done 11:31 AM

❯ Any other kinds of indeterminism we could face in statements?                 

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes. Beyond time and random bytes I see five kinds. The first is already in
  s4ql today.

  1. Which row, where the statement does not fix an order. s4ql deliberately
     draws a scan direction, so that nobody relies on an order we never
     promised. So update ... where id = (select id from t limit 1), or a
     subquery with ties, can pick a different row per run, and a write's effect
     depends on that draw. Last night's nightly showed it: one direction met a
     row that overflows, the other did not. Today the annotation does not record
     the draw or the value the subquery gave; the tests get it through a hook.
  2. The host's time zone. SQLite's 'localtime' modifier reads it. Tested just
     now: the same instant gives 10:00 under UTC and 19:00 under Tokyo. Either
     we refuse the modifier or it is an input of the host.
  3. State of the connection. changes(), total_changes(), and
     last_insert_rowid() depend on what the connection ran before, not on the
     database. None is supported, and I would keep it so.
  4. Results that depend on row order, once aggregates come: group_concat
     without an order, and a sum of reals, since floating-point addition depends
     on the order of its terms. With a drawn direction these could differ per
     run. To be checked when grouping is built; I have not tested it.
  5. The machine itself. Functions like sin or pow could differ in the last bit
     between platforms, and limits such as the maximum length decide whether a
     statement fails. Minor, since a commit records rows and replay never
     recomputes.

  How this bears on your open decision: kind 1 means that "all" is more than
  time and random bytes. For the log to say fully what a statement saw, the
  annotation would also need the value each subquery gave, or the directions
  drawn. Otherwise "all" is only "all of the clock and the dice".

✻ Crunched for 35s · done 11:32 AM

❯ Do subagent-research on whether this list is exhaustive, against what SQLite  
  supports (either from the docs or code or whatever)                           

⏺ Agent(Research non-determinism in SQLite statements) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A research agent is on it. It checks the list against primary sources, not
  memory:

  - SQLite's source (the 3.54.1 amalgamation in the repository): every built-in
    function that is not flagged deterministic, and the places that read state
    outside the statement, such as the clock, randomness, local time, the
    planner's statistics, and limits.
  - SQLite's documentation: the pages on deterministic functions, date and time,
    aggregates, select, quirks, and floating point, searched for what they call
    undefined or arbitrary.
  - Small experiments in the shell where a claim can be tested in seconds.

  For each kind it says whether it can change what a statement writes, what it
  returns, or only whether it fails, and whether s4ql can reach it or already
  shuts it out. The result becomes a research note in docs/research/. I report
  when it is back.

› Message from general-purpose
  No, the list of seven is not exhaustive, and item 1 is wrong in one case. The
  note is committed as `a19d1fbe` on branch `research-non-determinism`, not
  pushed: /Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a6e24c34cd354e
  58d/docs/research/non-determinism.md, with an entry added to
  docs/research/README.md.

  **Item 1 is wrong for a query that returns several rows.** SQLite fixes
  `'now'` for one call of `sqlite3_step()`, not for one statement.
  `sqlite3VdbeExec` resets `Vdbe.iCurrentTime` to 0 on every step, and
  lang_datefunc.html says "within the same sqlite3_step() call". If `'now'`'s
  arguments are constants, it is computed once per run, so no change shows. If
  they read a column, a 100,000-row select gave 41 different times. Writes, with
  or without `returning`, gave one value. s4ql's "once per statement" is still
  one of the answers SQLite can give.

  **Missing, most important first:**
  1. **The order in which a write visits rows decides what it writes or whether
  it fails.** Item 3 covers only returned rows. An upsert from `insert ...
  select` with two source rows of one key wrote 200, and with the order reversed
  100; `update ... from` did the same. quirks.html §15 says the success of
  `update set k = k + 1` "depends on an arbtrary ordering choice of the query
  planner".
  2. **Which rows get evaluated decides whether a statement fails.** `where
  abs(v) > 0 limit 1` returned row 1 in one order and failed with `integer
  overflow` in the other. This is reachable today with arithmetic and `limit`.
  3. **Which of several equal values comes back.** `max(x collate nocase)` of
  `'a'` and `'A'` returned either one, and `distinct` of `1` and `1.0` returned
  either type, depending on order. Bare columns take an "arbitrarily selected
  row" (lang_select.html).
  4. **Planner statistics.** A hand-written `sqlite_stat1` changed the index a
  query used, and with it the row order: `18,148,278,…` against `18,109,148,…`.
  s4ql shuts this out with fixed access rules and by rejecting `sqlite_` names.
  5. **The physical layout of the file.** `sqlite_offset()` (flagged
  deterministic in the source), `page_count`, `freelist_count`,
  `schema_version`, `data_version` and `rootpage` read the file, not the rows.
  Two caches built from the same commits can differ in them.
  6. **Random column names.** The fifth duplicate column name in a subquery,
  view or `create table ... as select` gets a random suffix (`a:4240065158`,
  then `a:279010675` in another run), from `sqlite3_randomness` in
  `sqlite3ColumnsFromExprList`.
  7. **Settings and failures of resources:** pragmas and db_config options,
  `sqlite3_limit`, heap limits, a full disk, interrupt, the progress handler,
  `SQLITE_BUSY`. Item 5 names only the three counters.
  8. **Integer `sum` overflow depends on order.** `max, 1, -1` failed with
  `integer overflow`; `max, -1, 1` returned the maximum.

  **Corrections within the list:**
  - **Item 6:** SQLite sums reals with compensated summation, so 20,000 values
  gave identical bits in either order. Order still decides overflow: `1e308,
  1e308, -1e308` gave `Inf`, the other order `1.0e+308`.
  - **Item 7:** 3.54.1 does not use `long double`, and its float/text conversion
  is its own code. The math functions call the host's `libm`, and Go's `math`
  differs from macOS's in the last bit for `ln(3)` and `tan(1.2)`. That matters
  for s4ql's evaluator in Go.
  - **Item 7:** `printf('%p', 255)` returns `FF`, so it does not leak a pointer.
  - **Item 5:** an insert into a `without rowid` table leaves
  `last_insert_rowid()` unchanged.

  The note also has a table of 28 kinds and of s4ql's own kinds. It lists every
  built-in function not flagged deterministic, with its macro and flags; no
  aggregate or window function carries the flag, except the JSON aggregates,
  which carry it although their result depends on input order. It ends with what
  I could not determine.

⏺ The list was not exhaustive, and one thing I told you earlier was wrong. The
  full note is in docs/research/non-determinism.md (28 kinds, each with its
  source).

  My error first. I said SQLite fixes 'now' for one statement, and that a select
  over 3,000,000 rows gave one time. That test was flawed: with constant
  arguments SQLite computes 'now' once and reuses it. The source and the
  documentation say the time is fixed per sqlite3_step() call, which for a query
  means per returned row. When the expression reads a column, a select of
  100,000 rows gave 41 different times. For writes it holds: one time per
  statement. s4ql's rule "once per statement" is stricter than SQLite, and it is
  still one of the answers SQLite can give.

  What was missing, most important first:

  1. The order in which a write visits rows decides what it writes. I had only
     listed the order of returned rows. An upsert fed by two source rows of one
     key wrote 200 in one order and 100 in the other. SQLite's own quirks page
     says the success of update t set k = k + 1 depends on an arbitrary choice
     of the planner.
  2. Which rows get evaluated decides whether a statement fails. Reachable in
     s4ql today with arithmetic and limit: one order returns a row, the other
     fails with an overflow.
  3. Which of several equal values comes back: max of 'a' and 'A' under nocase,
     distinct of 1 and 1.0.
  4. The physical file: sqlite_offset(), page counts, the schema version. Two
     caches built from the same commits can differ in them.
  5. Random column names that SQLite invents for duplicate names in a subquery.
  6. Settings and resource failures: pragmas, limits, a full disk, an interrupt.
  7. Integer sum overflows depending on order.

  Corrections to my list: SQLite sums reals with compensated summation, so order
  did not change the bits in the test, only overflow. And the math functions
  call the host's C library, where Go's results differ in the last bit for some
  inputs, which matters for our evaluator in Go.

  What it means for your open decision: "what a statement saw" is mostly the
  order in which it met the rows, and that is far more than time and random
  bytes.

✻ Sautéed for 20s · done 11:44 AM

❯ Hmm. So maybe the best solution right now is to not put it in the annotation  
  after all? We don't want to add anything and everything that could change.    
  That's in the rows themselves.                                                

⏺ I agree, and that is the cleaner design.

  The reasons, as I see them now:

  - A partial record is the worst of both. With time and random bytes but
    without the row order, the annotation would look complete and not be. You
    said all or nothing; "all" turns out to be unreachable in practice.
  - The rows are the truth and already hold the outcome. The time a statement
    used and the bytes it drew are in the rows it wrote, wherever they mattered.
  - The annotation gets a simple meaning: what the client sent. Text and
    parameters, nothing derived.
  - The tests do not need it. They can take a statement's time, random bytes,
    and subquery values from test hooks, as they do for subquery values today.

  It means the spec's sentence loses its second half. Today:

  ▎ Next to the results, a commit object stores the SQL statements that produced
  ▎ them, with their parameters and their non-deterministic inputs, such as the
  ▎ time and random bytes.

  Proposed:

  ▎ Next to the results, a commit object stores the SQL statements that produced
  ▎ them, with their parameters.

  The rest stays: "They are annotations, not the truth, and replay never depends
  on them."

  Shall I record the decision as "nothing" and make that change?

❯ Yes. Should we even drop the parameters?

⏺ Recorded and pushed: the annotation holds no time and no random bytes, and the
  spec's sentence now ends at "with their parameters".

  On dropping the parameters too: I think there is a real case for it, and it is
  the same argument you made about random bytes.

  What it costs to keep them. The format stores every parameter whole, by
  position. An insert passes its values as parameters, so each value sits in the
  commit twice: once in the row and once in the annotation. A goqite message
  with a 1 MB body would be a commit of about 2 MB. This is reasoning from the
  format; I have not measured real commits.

  What they add. For a write, almost nothing the rows do not already show: the
  new values are in the rows, and the key of an updated or deleted row is in its
  row change. What only the parameters show is a value in a where that is not
  stored, such as the cutoff in delete from t where created < ?.

  What stays if they go. The statement's text: which shape of statement made
  this commit. That is small, and it is what helps most when someone reads the
  log.

  What is lost. The log could no longer be read as a record of exactly what the
  client asked. Nothing in s4ql depends on that, and the tests can take the
  arguments from the calls they made themselves.

  So I lean to dropping them: the annotation becomes the statement's text only.
  One thing to be aware of: a literal written into the text stays in the log, a
  parameter does not.

  Do you want the parameters dropped?

✻ Cooked for 1s · done 12:12 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  I read the Antithesis post (fetched in full, dated 2026-11-24), the bbolt
  issue it links (#1335), the LazyFS paper, the fsyncgate thread, Rebello et al.
  (ATC 2020), and sqlite.org's pages on powersafe overwrite and on SQLite's own
  testing.

  **Modes the post describes:** power loss (writes that were never synced are
  lost), torn writes and torn sequences of writes, a full disk or no free
  inodes, I/O errors returned by syscalls, EIO on fsync where the pages are
  marked clean but never reach the disk (fsyncgate), and two latency faults:
  syscalls that complete out of order, and operations that a read sees half
  done. Memory pressure (pages evicted from the cache) only shows up as the
  trigger of its one public bug: bbolt treated a commit as done after a failed
  meta fsync, and later panicked on every open. Nothing else is named as caught.

  **What s4ql has:** none of these on the local disk inside `TestSimulation`.
  - The simulation's only cache failure is the buggify point in
  `Tx.commitCache`.
  - Its crash copies the files as the kernel holds them, which is a process
  kill, not a power loss.
  - Its workloads run with `synchronous = off` (`SyncOff`), so no sync is ever
  issued.
  - `internal/sqlite/faultvfs` returns errors (including `SQLITE_FULL` and
  `CANTOPEN`), but only in scenario tests, one fault at a time. Spool files fail
  only through `WrapSpools`.
  - The S3 side already injects the request-level equivalents: lost requests and
  answers, broken downloads, writes landing late, slowness.

  **A gap I measured:** with `synchronous = normal`, SQLite drops the error of
  an automatic checkpoint (`sqlite3WalDefaultHook` returns OK regardless). With
  every sync failing, all 200 commits succeeded while 199 syncs failed; with
  `synchronous = full`, all 200 failed. So a failed sync of the cache never
  stops the instance, which breaks the spec's rule for local I/O failures. I
  used a throwaway test for this and deleted it; it is not in the commit.

  **Harmless by design:** any loss that leaves the cache at an earlier commit of
  the log, because catch-up applies the rest. Nothing tests that claim under
  power loss, and the mutant that weakens the cache's sync still survives. It is
  not harmless if a page silently reverts while the instance keeps serving, for
  example after a failed writeback when the page is evicted.

  **Not needed:** out-of-order completion and half-done operations (the
  scheduler never has two goroutines inside SQLite), power loss of the spool
  files (they are unlinked), and memory pressure on its own.

  **Ranked proposals** (where to inject, what to check, and the planted bug that
  would prove the check):
  1. **Failed sync, then an eviction or power loss.** Needs a VFS that keeps
  "what reads see" and "what is on disk" apart. Planted bug:
  `ignoreCheckpointError`, once the core runs its own checkpoints. What the core
  should do with a failed checkpoint is a design question for Markus.
  2. **Random I/O errors and a full disk in `TestSimulation`,** through
  `faultvfs` and `WrapSpools`, judged by the existing checks and by
  `run.stopped`. Planted bug: a local failure during replay reported as
  `ErrLogBroken`.
  3. **Power loss tied to a disk operation** (before, during, after), with syncs
  on. After restart the cache must be at a commit of the log at or before the
  crash, with the model's rows. Planted bug: `synchronous = off`, the surviving
  mutant.
  4. **Torn writes inside one write,** as a variant of 3. This mostly retests
  SQLite.
  5. **A slow disk as time,** by a host sleep around calls into the cache. No
  planted bug found yet.

  **Commit:** `d5b5aba1` on `research-nightmare-mode`, not pushed. Files:
  - `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a80f70436fa0d4f2e/d
  ocs/research/nightmare-mode.md` (new)
  - `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a80f70436fa0d4f2e/d
  ocs/research/README.md` (index entry)
  - `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a80f70436fa0d4f2e/d
  ocs/todo/storage-core-tests.md` (the "read this post" item replaced with the
  ranked list)

⏺ The research on the Antithesis post is in and on main
  (docs/research/nightmare-mode.md), with a ranked list of disk failures to add
  to the simulator.

  The short version: the post describes power loss, torn writes, a full disk,
  I/O errors, and a failed fsync whose pages are quietly dropped. Our simulation
  has none of these on the local disk: its crash is a process kill, not a power
  loss, and its workloads run with syncing off. The S3 side is well covered.

  One finding goes beyond testing, and I am having it prepared as a design
  question for you. The agent measured that with SQLite's synchronous = normal,
  an error from an automatic checkpoint is dropped: with every sync failing, all
  200 commits still succeeded. So a failed sync of the cache does not stop the
  instance, which the spec's rule for local I/O failures says it should. The
  cache is rebuilt from the log, so the truth is safe; the open point is whether
  a read could be served from a cache that quietly differs from the log. I
  asked for the exact sequence, the options, and a recommendation before I bring
  it to you.

✻ Churned for 22s · done 12:15 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  Design question: a failed sync of the cache never stops the instance, and in
  one case it can lead to a wrong read. Tags: [M] measured, [S] read in SQLite's
  source or docs, [R] my reasoning.
  1. Rule: docs/spec.md:65, "A local I/O failure stops the instance. If reading
  or writing the local cache fails, the instance stops serving for good." Line
  66: recovery is a normal start from the recorded position, and a cache that
  cannot be opened is not rebuilt. docs/todo/wal-index.md:3 adds: "The failure
  must arrive as an error for that." (That todo is about the memory-mapped
  -shm.) Nothing in docs/todo covers syncs, except my item in
  storage-core-tests.md.
  2. Production uses WAL mode with `synchronous = normal`, set in `conn.setUp`
  (internal/core/cache.go:271; WAL is set in `conn.wal`, :377). A caller cannot
  change it: `syncOff` is unexported and only tests set it (core.go:82), and
  s4ql.Open passes `core.Options{}` (s4ql.go:385). Checkpoints are SQLite's
  automatic ones, at the default threshold of 1000 pages. The core never runs
  one.
  3. What happens. With normal, a commit only writes() WAL frames; the only
  fsyncs come at a checkpoint (pragma.html) [S]. The automatic checkpoint runs
  after a commit. Its fsync(WAL) gets EIO, and `sqlite3WalDefaultHook` drops the
  result and returns SQLITE_OK (sqlite3.c ~190856) [S]: 200 commits succeeded
  while 199 syncs failed [M]. The checkpoint stops before writing the db file,
  and nBackfill stays where it was [S, walCheckpoint]. Afterwards, on ext4/XFS
  the failed WAL pages are marked clean, keep their new content in memory, and
  hold old bytes on disk (Rebello et al., ATC'20) [S].
   Saved by design [R]: if power is lost, the WAL's checksum chain truncates at
  the first bad frame, so the cache comes back at an earlier commit of the log
  and catch-up applies the rest. The commits involved are already in S3, because
  the cache is never ahead.
   Wrong read [R, not run]: (a) the sync fails as above, and the instance serves
  on; (b) memory pressure evicts a clean, unpersisted WAL page; (c) a read
  reads that frame. `sqlite3WalReadFrame` is a plain read with no checksum check
  (sqlite3.c:70612) [S], so it gets old bytes from an earlier WAL generation. A
  malformed page gives CORRUPT and a stop, which is fine. A well-formed old
  page gives wrong rows served under a newer position. (d) The next checkpoint's
  fsync then succeeds (fsyncgate: nothing is dirty any more). It copies the
  reverted frame into the db file and syncs it, so the wrong page becomes
  durable and survives restarts. Later replay may hit ErrLogBroken or diverge
  silently. So yes, a wrong-read sequence can be constructed. It needs an EIO on
  writeback followed by an eviction before the next checkpoint. It is the bbolt
  bug's pattern.
  4. Options:
   a) `synchronous = full`: every commit fsyncs, so the EIO reaches `classify`
  and the instance stops. Cost: an fsync per commit, and the comment in setUp
  calls that sync useless. Not covered: a failed sync in an automatic checkpoint
  is still dropped, and a restart without a reboot reads unpersisted frames
  through the page cache, which can still be evicted later [R].
   b) The core turns automatic checkpoints off (`wal_autocheckpoint=0`) and runs
  `pragma wal_checkpoint` itself, sending errors through classify, so the
  instance stops. Cost: small, plus a decision on when to run them and on whom
  (the writer, after a commit). Not covered: a writeback that fails before any
  checkpoint, and an eviction before the stop. The window is the time between
  checkpoints. The reopen after the stop has the same restart-without-reboot
  caveat as a).
   c) After a stop for a local failure, the next open rebuilds the cache from
  the log. This would change spec line 66. Cost: a full replay. It closes the
  restart caveat but not the running window.
   d) Verify the cache against the log, for example with page checksums
  (cksumvfs, SQLITE_IOERR_DATA) [S]. Not covered: a page that reverts to an
  older valid version keeps a valid checksum [R]. A full comparison against the
  log is expensive.
   e) Accept it and reword the rule to say that failures SQLite does not report
  are out of scope. Free, but a wrong read stays possible.
  5. Recommendation: b) now. It is cheap, it makes the rule true for every error
  SQLite can report, and it gives a planted bug (`ignoreCheckpointError`). Add
  c) if Markus wants the restart case closed. Before deciding on d), build the
  VFS of proposal 1 (separate volatile and durable state, with eviction) and see
  whether the sequence in 3 really produces a wrong read.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ fabrik:builder(Core runs its own checkpoints) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

› Message from general-purpose
  I sent the design question to "main" in 25 lines; I committed nothing. A
  sequence that produces a wrong read can be built on paper, but I have not run
  it.

  1. **The rule:** `docs/spec.md:65` says a local I/O failure stops the
  instance. Line 66 says recovery is a normal start and the cache is not rebuilt
  automatically. The only related todo is `docs/todo/wal-index.md:3`: "The
  failure must arrive as an error for that."
  2. **The setting:** a production instance uses WAL with `synchronous =
  normal`, set at `internal/core/cache.go:271`. A caller cannot change it:
  `syncOff` is unexported and only tests set it, and `s4ql.Open` passes
  `core.Options{}`. Checkpoints are SQLite's automatic ones; the core never runs
  one.
  3. **The failure:**
     - **What fails:** the fsync of the WAL during an automatic checkpoint.
  SQLite drops the error (`sqlite3WalDefaultHook`), which I measured: 200
  commits succeeded while 199 syncs failed.
     - **When the design saves us:** after a power loss, the cache comes back at
  an earlier commit and catches up from the log.
     - **The wrong read:** a failed writeback, then the kernel evicts that page
  from memory, then a read of the WAL frame. SQLite reads frames without
  checking their checksum (`sqlite3WalReadFrame`, read in source), so it gets
  old bytes. If those form a valid page, the instance serves wrong rows. The
  next checkpoint's sync then succeeds and writes the old page into the db file
  for good.
  4. **The options:** `synchronous = full`; the core runs its own checkpoints
  and checks their result; rebuild the cache after a stop for a local failure;
  page checksums; accept it and reword the rule. The message gives the cost of
  each and what it leaves uncovered.
  5. **My recommendation:** the core runs its own checkpoints now, optionally
  with the rebuild after a stop. Build the fault VFS first, to see whether the
  wrong-read sequence really happens.

⏺ Agent "Research Antithesis nightmare mode" finished · 12m 25s

⏺ The finding is now exact. Here is where it stands and what I did; nothing
  needs your decision right now.

  The rule we have (spec): "A local I/O failure stops the instance."

  What is true today. A production instance runs the cache in WAL mode with
  synchronous = normal and lets SQLite checkpoint on its own. In that mode the
  only syncs happen at a checkpoint, and SQLite's automatic checkpoint drops its
  error. Measured: with every sync failing, 200 commits succeeded. So this kind
  of local failure does not stop the instance, and the rule is not kept.

  How bad. The truth is never at risk: every commit is in S3 before the cache
  has it, and after a power loss the cache comes back at an earlier commit and
  catches up. But a wrong read can be built on paper: a failed writeback, then
  the kernel evicts that page, then SQLite reads the old bytes without checking
  them, and a later checkpoint makes the old page permanent. That needs an I/O
  error followed by an eviction, and it is reasoned from SQLite's source, not
  reproduced.

  What I started. Making the code keep the agreed rule is a detail, so I did not
  wait: a builder is making the core run the checkpoints itself and stop the
  instance when one fails, with a planted bug to prove the check. That closes
  every error SQLite can report.

  What may come to you later. Whether a stop for a local failure should rebuild
  the cache on the next start (the spec says it does not), and whether the cache
  needs verifying against the log. I want the wrong-read sequence reproduced in
  a fault-injecting file layer before I bring you either, so you decide on
  evidence.

❯ Maybe we should just be stricter than synchronous = normal?                   

⏺ Probably yes, and it fits with what the builder is doing. It is not a
  replacement for it.

  What synchronous = full gives. Every commit to the cache syncs the WAL, so a
  failing sync surfaces at that commit and the instance stops. The research
  measured exactly this: with every sync failing, all 200 commits failed under
  full, where all 200 succeeded under normal. The window in which unsynced data
  sits only in memory shrinks from "until the next checkpoint" to one commit.

  What it does not give. The checkpoint itself also syncs, and SQLite's
  automatic checkpoint still drops that error under full. So the core running
  its own checkpoints is needed either way. The two together mean every sync
  error SQLite sees stops the instance.

  What neither gives. After a stop for a failed sync, a restart without a reboot
  starts from the same cache file, which may hold pages that never reached the
  disk. That is the question of rebuilding the cache after such a stop, which
  the spec today says we do not.

  The cost. One sync per commit to the cache. For a client's commit that is
  small beside the write to S3 it already waits for. The place it could hurt is
  catching up on a long log, where every replayed commit would sync. I have no
  measurement of either.

  I would take full together with the own checkpoints, if the cost on catch-up
  is acceptable. The checkpoint builder is already measuring cost; I can have it
  measure normal against full for commits and for catch-up and bring you the
  numbers. Or do you want full regardless of the cost?

  The statement pass for `||`, `lower` and `hex` is built and committed on
  branch `text-stmt`, last commit `61c12b96`, not pushed. `make test`, `make
  lint`, the crosscheck module, `TestDifferential -sequences 400` (338 s), and
  FuzzCheck 180 s, FuzzGenerate 2×60 s and FuzzSplit 60 s pass; the new tests
  pass with `-count=20` and `-race`.

  - **New kinds:** `stmt.ExprConcat` (two operands) and `stmt.ExprCall` (one
  argument), with a new field `Expression.Function` (`FuncLower`, `FuncHex`).
  `ExprValue` may now hold Text, or Blob as hex's argument. They stand where
  arithmetic stands: SET values and either side of a WHERE comparison, in
  subqueries too. `||` binds tighter than `*`; calls and `||` count depth as
  SQLite does, checked at both edges, and a call's parenthesis counts toward the
  limit of 100.
  - **Refusals:** each name follows what SQLite does, all in
  `/docs/todo/text-functions.md`:
    - other functions are `function other than lower or hex`, at the name;
    - `wrong number of arguments` and `* as an argument`: SQLite says "wrong
  number of arguments" only once it has found the tables, after `no such table`,
  so the pass cannot give SQLite's error there;
    - DISTINCT/ALL (SQLite takes them), ORDER BY, FILTER and OVER (SQLite
  refuses them late), and names in brackets or backticks; double quotes are
  taken, as SQLite takes them;
    - `text in arithmetic`, `number|NULL|blob in a concatenation` (SQLite's `1
  || 2` is '12'), `number|NULL|blob as an argument` (hex takes a blob; SQLite
  converts, and `hex(null)` is '');
    - parentheses around a call or around its argument;
    - a text operand's place is refused before its contents: `lower(1) + 2` is
  `text in arithmetic`.
  - **Outside `internal/sql/stmt`:**
    - `internal/expr/expr.go`: `expr.Check` refuses the new kinds at the root; a
  malformed node is an internal error.
    - `internal/engine/expression.go`: new `textRefusal`, called in `Check`,
  `prepare` and `execPart` (ExecAll), after the text and argument checks and
  before the database; `leftmostOffset` stops at a call.
    - `internal/engine/plan.go`: `formatArithmetic` writes the new kinds.
    - Tests: `TestEngine_text`, `TestLeftmostOffset`, four FuzzSQL seeds,
  `pair.checkArithmetic` holds the engine to `stmttest.TextRefusal`; FuzzExpr
  seeds and a branch for text; a `TestArithmetic` subtest in `conn_test.go`.
    - Small additions to the engine and expr READMEs. The driver's Prepare now
  refuses text.
  - **Faults found in existing code:**
    - `stmttest.ExpressionText` panicked on a call; fixed.

  supported since arithmetic; FuzzGenerate found it. I fixed the check and kept
  the input as a seed. By reading, the fault dates from arithmetic; I did not
  run it on `main`.
  - **For the evaluator and engine steps:** SQLite keeps resolving names after
  an error inside a call's arguments, and a later error wins: `hex(nosuch) =
  nosuch2` gives `no such column: nosuch2`, where `missingName` stops at the
  first. That is why the engine refuses text up front for now; that step must
  copy SQLite's order or keep refusing.
    - `lower` folds ASCII only.
    - `hex(NULL)` is '' (not NULL), and hex doubles the length.
    - Numeric, blob and wrongly classed columns or arguments must be refused by
  type, and so must plain values of another class compared with text (`lower(v)
  > 1` is true for every non-NULL `v` in SQLite).

  which a call lacks.

⏺ Yes. The narrowest way is a sudoers rule that lets the maragubot user run
  exactly this one command without a password, and nothing else as root.

  You would install it once (it needs your password):

  /etc/sudoers.d/fseventsd-restart
  sudo chmod 440 /etc/sudoers.d/fseventsd-restart
  sudo visudo -c

  After that I can run sudo -n /usr/bin/killall fseventsd myself. Any other sudo
  still asks for a password, which I checked I do not have today.

  What it opens up: any process running as maragubot could restart that daemon.
  That is harmless, since launchd brings it straight back, as we just saw.

  If you install it, I would do two things on my side: add that exact command to
  the project's allowed commands so it does not prompt, and change the entry in
  AGENTS.md to say that the lead restarts the daemon itself when it passes

› Message from general-purpose
  No confirmed fault loses or corrupts what the log in S3 holds. One confirmed
  fault does acknowledge a transaction whose own swap never applied, which is a
  lost update. Each of the four confirmed faults has a test that fails on every
  run (10 out of 10).

  **Confirmed faults** (tests named `TestAdversarial_*` in
  `internal/core/adversarial_test.go`):
  1. **Two identical transactions both acknowledged** (codex): a and b start at
  the same parent, make the same change and read the same wall clock, so their
  commit objects have the same name. a's swap gets no answer, b takes the lease
  and commits, and a's `Tx.inLog` finds "its" object in the log and returns nil.
  Both `Commit` calls return nil from one swap. Reproduced on the scheduler
  with `StopWall` on both instances; the store ends with only 2 commit objects.
  It needs two wall clocks that read the same nanosecond.
  2. **Takeover on a shared cache stops the new holder** (codex): a's swap
  applies, then a stalls longer than its lease before committing the cache. b
  takes over, reads the old position and plans the replay; a then commits the
  cache; b's replay finds the cache already at the commit and stops for good
  with `ErrLogBroken`. Reproduced with a host that sleeps at the yield between
  the swap and the cache. Only availability is lost; this setup is supported
  (`TestOpen_cacheDir`).
  3. **A stale read after the cache file is replaced** (agy): the cache files of
  a running holder are deleted and another instance opens on the same path,
  making a new empty cache. The holder's next `DB.Reader` opens that file and
  serves `EmptyLog` as fresh. Reproduced in a synctest bubble. It needs an
  operator error.
  4. **Overflow in the wait for a lease** (agy): `Options.Watch` has no upper
  bound. With a manifest lease of 9e18 ns (about 285 years), `left + margin` in
  `DB.acquire` overflows and the lease is taken at once. Reproduced. No s4ql
  instance writes such a lease, so there is no effect in practice.

  **Counts by class:**
  - codex (gpt-6.2-sol, medium), 4 findings: 2 confirmed, 0 plausible, 2 true on
  paper, 0 already handled, 0 wrong.
  - agy (Gemini 4 Carbon, high), 10 findings counted as 12 claims because its
  #10 has three parts: 2 confirmed, 0 plausible, 8 true on paper, 1 already
  recorded (the `-shm` mmap crash in `docs/todo/wal-index.md`, counted as
  already handled), 1 wrong (clock checks per statement are by design).
  - The two models found nothing in common.
  - codex refused two runs as a "possible cybersecurity risk", the second even
  with "adversarial" and "attack" removed from the prompt. A resumed session
  that asked only for its final answer worked. agy took about 45 minutes and
  changed no file.

  **What I recommend:**
  - Fault 1 needs a format decision from Markus. A random value in the commit
  header would make every name unique.
  - Faults 2 to 4 are details for a builder:
    - replay takes a cache already at the commit as caught up;
    - a read checks its position against the one the write connection last
  committed;
    - `Watch` gets an upper bound.
  - Cheap hardening from the on-paper claims:
    - on `ErrExists` at upload, check the checksum of the object that is there;
    - at `Open`, refuse a cache at `EmptyLog` that holds tables, and one that is
  not UTF-8;
    - check the 250 key columns in replay;
    - check for a stop between rows in `Tx.scan`;
    - fix `docs/todo/s3-client.md`, which says a store that checks no payload
  hash "still works".

  alone, or merge the tests together with their fixes.

  1. Two identical transactions can both be acknowledged. Two instances start
     from the same commit, make the same change, and read the same wall-clock
     nanosecond, so their commit objects get the same name. One loses the lease,
     finds "its" object in the log, and reports success. One swap, two

✻ Cogitated for 1m 15s · done 1:09 PM

⏺ Agent "Annotation holds the text only" is waiting on its own background work ·
  1h 12m 34s

› Message from general-purpose
  Fault 1 (two identical transactions both acknowledged): I recommend a random
  nonce in the commit header; the details follow. [R] = read in code or docs,
  [M] = measured, [J] = my reasoning.
  1. The name [R]. It is the SHA-256 of every byte of the commit object, hashed
  as the bytes are written (`format/writer.go:108`, `:335`, `:278` `Close`). The
  key is `commits/<first 2 hex>/<name>` (`core/core.go:372`). What gets hashed:
  the header (`version`, `parent`, `commit_time` from `d.host.Wall()`,
  `tx.go:1109`; schema `proto/s4ql/v1`, `Header` at line 41), the net changes,
  and the annotations (SQL text without parameters, spec line 54). Spec line 52:
  "named by the SHA-256 checksum of its content, with no metadata in the name…
  a reader can verify every commit object against its name". `tx.go:1499` gives
  the reason for `ErrExists` = success: "An object that is there already can
  only be this commit object, left by an upload that reported a failure…
  harmless, because the object is named by its content and created only if
  absent." The header carries the time "for a later feature, such as restoring
  to a point in time" (`format/README.md`, The commit time).
  2. `Tx.inLog` [R] (`tx.go:1658`). It walks from the new head back to
  `tx.parent` and returns published when `at == name` (`tx.go:1663`). It
  compares the name and nothing else. It runs only from `publish`
  (`tx.go:1638`): the lease is lost and an attempt of the swap had no answer
  (`f.unknown`), so the swap may have landed before the new holder took over.
  That is the spec's "committed iff its commit object is in the log".
  3. What tells writers apart today [R]. The header holds nothing about the
  writer. The manifest's lease holds `holder`, a per-instance ID of 128 random
  bits drawn at `Open` (`core.go:301`, `h.Uint64()` twice), plus duration,
  renewal time and a salt that is new at every write. There is no generation or
  epoch. Two instances share an ID only by a 2^-128 chance [J]. But the manifest
  is overwritten, so nothing in the log records who wrote a commit.
  4. When it happens [R+J]. Both transactions must have the same parent, the
  same net changes with the same values in the same order, and the same
  annotation texts. With parameterised SQL, two runs of the same job meet all of
  that. They must also read the same wall-clock nanosecond. b takes over only
  after a's lease has run out plus the margin, so b's spool comes at least the
  margin (2 s or more) after a's, and b's wall clock must lag a's by exactly
  that elapsed time. [M] On this Mac, `time.Now` moves in whole microseconds
  (smallest step 1000 ns, `ns%1000` always 0). Linux is not measured: [J] vDSO
  `CLOCK_REALTIME`, nanosecond resolution. With clocks that run on two machines
  it practically never happens. It needs a wall clock that stands still or is
  coarse and coincides. In the simulation it does not happen today, because
  every workload transaction annotates a random ID (`simulation_test.go:847`);
  only my test with `StopWall` on both instances gets it.
  Worst case: two increments read 0, both write 1, and both `Commit` calls
  return nil. The counter ends at 1, so one acknowledged update is silently
  lost. The same goes for two withdrawals of 10 from 100, which leave 90. No
  error appears anywhere. The log and its replay are consistent; serializability
  is broken.
  5. Options:
     - (a) Random value in the header, e.g. 16 bytes from `host.Uint64` drawn in
  `spoolCommit`. Format: one header field. Version 0 breaks freely, and readers
  refuse unknown fields, so old caches and logs are refused (allowed by
  AGENTS.md). Cost: about 18 bytes per commit. It stays deterministic in the
  simulation. A retry still finds its own object: [R] every upload retry and
  swap within one `Commit` re-sends the same spooled bytes (`tx.go:1513`), and
  nothing re-spools. Names are then unique per transaction [J].
     - (b) The writer's ID (`d.id`) in the header. Different instances can no
  longer collide, and retries are unaffected. Within one instance, I believe [J]
  two identical objects cannot both be unresolved between parent and head:
  there is one writer lock, and the instance does not serve while an outcome is
  unknown. That rests on an argument rather than on construction. Cost: 32
  bytes. It also records who wrote each commit, which (a) does not. A lease
  generation would need a new manifest field and a counter; the manifest has
  none today and the salt replaced the counter on purpose (`format/README.md`).
     - (c) `inLog` compares something that only the writer knows. That something
  has to be inside the object, since the name is all a walk sees, so this
  reduces to (a) or (b). Putting it in the key or the name breaks spec line 52.
     - (d) Leave it, and document that commit time plus content must differ.
  That rests on clocks, which the spec otherwise keeps out of writes.
  6. My recommendation is (a): a random 128-bit value in the header. It makes
  names unique by construction, keeps the retry property and spec line 52, and
  costs one field. Add (b) only if a record of who wrote each commit is wanted
  for its own sake. It is a format change, so it is Markus's call.

⏺ One decision for you, on the commit format. It comes from the confirmed fault
  that two identical transactions can both be acknowledged.

  How it happens. A commit object is named by the SHA-256 of its content: the
  parent, the commit time, the row changes, and the statements' text. When a
  writer loses its lease while a swap of its own has no answer, it looks in the
  log for an object with its name to learn whether its commit landed. If another
  instance made the very same change from the same parent at the same
  wall-clock instant, that object has the same name, and the first writer takes
  it for its own.

  What goes wrong. Two increments read 0, both write 1, both are told success,
  and the counter ends at 1. One acknowledged update is silently lost. The log
  itself stays consistent.

  How likely. It needs the same rows, the same statements, and the same clock
  reading. On macOS the clock moves in whole microseconds (measured). With two
  real machines it practically never happens; with a coarse or stopped clock it
  can. So it is rare, but correctness then rests on clocks, which the design
  otherwise avoids for writes.

  Options:

  - (a) A random 128-bit value in each commit's header. Names become unique by
    construction. About 18 bytes per commit. A retried upload still finds its
    own object, because a retry sends the same bytes.
  - (b) The writer's instance ID in the header. Also separates two instances,
    and records who wrote each commit. 32 bytes. Within one instance it rests on
    an argument, not on construction.
  - (c) Leave it and document it.

  The spec's rule that a commit object is named by its content, with no metadata
  in the name, holds under (a) and (b).

  I recommend (a). Which do you want?

✻ Brewed for 7s · done 1:10 PM

  I sent the follow-up on fault 1 (two identical transactions both acknowledged)

  - **Recommendation:** put a random 128-bit value in the commit header. Names
  then become unique by construction. A retried upload still finds its own
  object, because every retry within one `Commit` sends the same spooled bytes
  (`tx.go:1513`). It costs one field in format version 0, and it is a format
  change, so it is Markus's decision.
  - **Alternative:** the writer's ID in the header would also separate
  instances. Within one instance it relies on an argument, not on construction.
  - **Measured:** on this Mac, Go's wall clock moves in whole microseconds
  (smallest step 1000 ns). I did not measure Linux.
  - **How likely:** it practically never happens with real clocks on two
  machines. The second writer spools at least the lease margin (2 s or more)
  after the first, so its wall clock would have to lag by exactly that, to the
  nanosecond. The simulation's workloads cannot produce it, because every
  transaction annotates a random ID.
  - **Worst case:** two increments from 0 both return nil and the counter ends
  at 1. One acknowledged update is lost without any error.

⏺ Yes, I have three, in the order I would do them. First what the numbers say.

  What it costs and gives today (measured):
  - The engine's round for arithmetic: 2,488 mutants, about 5 hours at 6
    workers, then 3 more hours to rerun survivors on the final code. The other
    two layers took about 3.5 hours each.

    file churn is what feeds fseventsd.

    fault in s4ql, and one planted bug that was never really planted. Useful,
    but mostly it strengthens tests. The adversarial review found four confirmed
    faults in about an hour.

  Suggestions:

  1. Move it to CI, sharded. Mutants are independent, so 20 runners finish in a
     twentieth of the time. The repository is public, so GitHub's runners are
     free. A scheduled workflow runs the mutants and publishes the survivors; a
     builder only handles survivors. This machine is freed, and fseventsd gets
     its quiet back.
  2. Batch it by time, not by feature. One round a week over everything that
     changed since the last round (the runner already selects by diff), instead
     of one round per layer of every feature. A feature counts as done after its
     generated checks, planted bugs, and night-sized runs. That also ends the
     repeated merges and re-checks of the list of equivalent mutants.
  3. Make each mutant cheaper, later and only after measuring where the time
     goes: compile all mutants into one binary and switch them on by a variable,
     so there is one build instead of thousands; and run only the tests that
     cover the mutated line. In the engine's round 462 of 2,488 mutants were
     spent only to learn they do not compile.

  I recommend 1 and 2 together, and leaving 3 until the first CI runs show
  whether it is still needed.

  Do you want me to go that way: mutation testing as a weekly sharded CI run,
  off the path of each feature?

⏺ No, on reflection it is not, and I have changed it to every night.

  Weekly was caution on my part. At our pace of many pull requests a day, a
  week's round would be thousands of mutants and one very long list of
  survivors, found long after the feature was built. A nightly round over only
  what changed since the last one stays small, and its survivors arrive while
  the work is fresh. The runs cost nothing on a public repository.

  Two things the builder now takes care of:

  - No repeated noise. If nobody has worked through last night's list yet, the
    next night updates the same issue. A night with no changed code does
    nothing.
  - Not starving other CI. GitHub limits how many jobs an account runs at once,
    and our agents open pull requests at all hours. The builder checks the limit
    and picks the hour and the number of runners so that pull request checks
    and the nightly tests are not held up.

  It can also be started by hand right after a feature lands.
