› Message from fabrik:builder
  The core now runs the cache's checkpoints itself, and a failed checkpoint or a
  failed sync of the write-ahead log at a commit stops the instance for good.
  Production runs `synchronous = full`, as the product owner decided. Everything
  passes except where noted below. Branch `core-checkpoints`, last commit
  `3ceb9d6a` (main merged in; I resolved one conflict in
  `docs/todo/storage-core-tests.md`). Not pushed.

  - **Who and when:** `pragma wal_autocheckpoint = 0` is set on every
  connection, readers too. The writer, under the writer lock, runs `pragma
  wal_checkpoint(noop)` after each commit (after `Tx.commitCache`, and after
  each commit object of a catch-up). When the log holds 1000 frames or more, it
  runs a passive checkpoint. That is exactly when and how SQLite's automatic
  checkpoint ran, minus the dropped error. Close runs no checkpoint of the core;
  SQLite's own checkpoint at the last close keeps the log if it fails, and the
  next open recovers it. A spare never writes, so it never checkpoints.
  - **What stops and what doesn't:** a checkpoint held back by readers, busy
  because another connection checkpoints, or refused as `SQLITE_LOCKED` while a
  statement is open is no failure; the next commit checkpoints. Any other error
  stops the instance with `ErrLocal`, and the commit still returns nil
  (`committed_and_stopped`), since it is in the log.
  - **Unbounded WAL:** the log grows only by what is committed while the oldest
  read transaction is open, as under SQLite before. After the read ends, the
  next commit's checkpoint copies the whole log and the commit after it starts
  the log again. Restart or truncate checkpoints are not needed: they would make
  commits wait for readers and bound nothing more.
  - **Tests:** `TestDB_checkpoint` (8 subtests) and `TestDB_sync`, both using
  faultvfs, no real time. The test of the research note now stops at the first
  commit, which is still acknowledged. A restart finds every commit, and
  replaying into an empty cache gives the same rows. They pass with `-count=20`
  and with `-race -count=20`.
  - **Planted bug `ignoreCheckpointError`:** a new simulation cell runs with
  syncs on and a checkpoint every 4 frames, and fails a sync of the cache file
  or of the log, two faults per node. `run.close` requires at most one failed
  sync per open and a `SQLITE_IOERR_FSYNC` stop for it. At 1000 seeds, 681 seeds
  found the bug (first ones 2 3 5 6 8 9 11 12 13 14), with none of the control
  and none without the bug.
  - **Cost (Mac Mini, load 17 to 25, six alternating runs each):**

  | | `normal` | `full` |
  |---|---|---|
  | Commits of 10 rows, per second | 1916 / 2075 | 2091 / 1880 |
  | Catch-up of 3000 commits | 0.49 s / 0.50 s | 0.66 s / 0.57 s |

    First figure is on `/Volumes/990`, second on the main disk. No difference
  was significant. The core's checkpoints cost the same as SQLite's automatic
  ones: 570 µs against 561 µs per commit. Catch-up applies one cache transaction
  per commit object, so `full` costs one sync per commit object. Whether to
  batch catch-up is recorded as open, not built.
  - **Linux caveat:** SQLite on macOS uses plain `fsync`, not `F_FULLFSYNC`, so
  these numbers understate `full`. CI and the nightly run on Linux, where every
  test outside the core's simulation now syncs at each commit, and that cost is
  unmeasured. Here the engine package took 329 s against 299 s at main.
  - **Fault found in self-review:** both reviewers found that a commit made from
  inside a scan loop stopped the instance as a "local I/O failure", though
  nothing failed. SQLite refuses any checkpoint with `SQLITE_LOCKED` while a
  statement is open. That case now counts as busy, and a test covers it. I also
  fixed the doc errors both reviewers found (SQLite syncs the cache file only at
  a checkpoint that copies the whole log), a committed `BUGCOUNT` placeholder,
  and the two simulation checks, which were too loose.
  - **Other runs:** core and engine without `-short`, engine `TestDifferential
  -sequences 400` (726 s), engine `TestSimulation` at 100 seeds (332 s), `make
  test` and `make lint` all pass. I did not run the engine's planted bugs at
  1000 seeds.
  - **For the product owner:**
    - After a stop for a local failure, SQLite's own checkpoint at the last
  close still tries once more in place, against "nothing is tried again in
  place". Turning it off needs `SQLITE_DBCONFIG_NO_CKPT_ON_CLOSE` in both
  bindings, including the browser one, and a change to the tests' `leftOpen`
  check.
    - The research note called a failed checkpoint "a design question for
  Markus"; this branch answers it with "stop".
  - **Record:** decisions, Markus's words, numbers and open points are in
  `docs/todo/cache-checkpoints.md` (new, indexed in `docs/todo.md`), not in
  `storage-core-tests.md`, where I removed the closed item. The core's README is
  updated. The diary has three steps.
  - **Scratch:** my logs are in `/Volumes/990/s4ql/core-checkpoints/`; delete
  that directory after the merge.

⏺ Agent(Research commit identity in other systems) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A research agent is on it, with primary sources only. It looks at how others
  learn whether a commit landed after an ambiguous answer, and how they keep two
  distinct transactions apart:

  - FoundationDB's unknown commit result and its idempotency IDs
  - Delta Lake, Iceberg, and similar table formats on object storage
  - log stores on S3 such as SlateDB, and LiteFS and Litestream
  - content-addressed stores such as Git and Dolt, where identical content
    sharing a name is intended
  - Kafka's idempotent producer, and request tokens in DynamoDB, CockroachDB,
    and Spanner

  For each it records whether a transaction's identity is random, derived from
  the writer, or derived from content, where it is stored, and how the writer
  uses it afterwards. It also searches for anyone who hit our exact fault, and
  says what follows for (a), (b), (c), or a fourth option.

  The result becomes a note in docs/research/. I come back with it and your lean
  to (a) noted.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The evaluator in `internal/expr` now computes `||`, `lower` and `hex`, and
  checks them against SQLite. The engine still refuses every statement with text
  before it reaches the evaluator. `make test`, `make lint`, `-race`, and
  `-count=20` of the new tests all pass. Branch `text-expr`, last commit
  `f52fac9e`, not pushed; worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a2d3581f117d3114a`.

  1. **Types and refusals at `Check`/`Bind`.** New types `TypeText` and
  `TypeBlob`. `||` and `lower` take text; `hex` takes text or a blob (text is
  the hex of its UTF-8 bytes). Refused by column type at `Check` and by argument
  at `Bind`, each at its offset: numbers or blobs in `||`/`lower` ("column of
  type INT in a concatenation", "argument 1 in a call of lower, which is a
  blob"), and numbers in `hex`. A TEXT or BLOB column alone now has its column's
  type instead of being refused.
  2. **NULL, same as SQLite:** `NULL || 'a'` and `'a' || NULL` are NULL,
  `lower(NULL)` is NULL, `hex(NULL)` is `''`. A NULL argument gives `TypeNull`
  for `||` and `lower`, and `TypeText` for `hex`.
  3. **New error `expr.ErrTooLong`**, where SQLite gives `string or blob too
  big`:
     - `||` whose result is longer than the limit;
     - `lower` of a text as long as the limit or longer;
     - `hex` when 2n+1 exceeds the limit;
     - at `Bind`, a text or blob literal longer than the limit. For the blob
  literal this is stricter than SQLite, which reads it as NULL.

     `Bind(args, limit)` now takes the limit, clamped to 30..1e9 as SQLite does.
  `*expr.Error` has a new `Function` field. No other evaluation error can come
  from the three.
  4. **Allocations:** arithmetic still allocates nothing. A text operation
  allocates its result once, or not at all when it returns an operand or empty
  text. `'m_' || lower(hex(x))` costs 2, or 3 when the hex digits contain A–F.
  Values share bytes with the row; Go strings never change, so a caller can keep
  a value after the row is gone. `Value` grew from 16 to 32 bytes; the
  arithmetic benchmark could not be compared with `main` because machine load
  was 27.
  5. **Planted faults:** all 8 caught by both `TestDifferential` and the
  `FuzzExpr` seeds (non-ASCII fold, lower-case hex, swapped `||`, `hex(NULL)` as
  NULL, NULL `||` returning the other operand, each of the three limits off by
  one).
  6. **Fuzz run:** 5 minutes, 5.2M executions, no failure. I committed the 47
  corpus inputs that contain text as seeds.
  7. **Changed outside `internal/expr`:**
     - `bindArithmetic` in `internal/engine/expression.go` passes `math.MaxInt`
  as the limit.
     - Two stale comments: one in `internal/engine/expression.go`, one in
  `internal/engine/README.md`.
     - Four keys in `internal/sql/mutation/equivalent.txt`, which my rewrite
  broke (`TestEquivalent` failed): two removed, two renamed, one added.
  8. **For the engine step** (details in `docs/todo/text-functions.md`, "The
  evaluator"):
     - Pass the database's real limit to `Bind`.
     - Map `ErrTooLong` to SQLite's own too-big error, since that is SQLite's
  answer. `errors.go` (root), `stmttest.EvaluationOf` and `stmttest.Quirk` don't
  handle it yet.
     - SQLite computes constant subtrees before any row: `set s = ?1 || ?2 || s`

  Details I decided, in a line each:

  - hex takes text as well as blobs (the hex of its bytes is no conversion
    quirk); numbers are refused everywhere SQLite would convert them.
  - Null is as in SQLite: null || 'a' is null, and hex(null) is the empty text.
  - A result longer than SQLite's length limit is an error, as it is in SQLite.

  I did not send a separate reviewer over it, in line with the resource rule:
  the evaluator is checked against SQLite directly, and the engine step that
  makes it reachable gets its review.

✻ Brewed for 20s · done 2:09 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  **Short answer:** I found no system that identifies a commit attempt only by
  its content and also promises a separate acknowledgement for each attempt. The
  systems that check after an ambiguous result whether their own commit landed
  all look for a value that only that attempt carries, and nearly all make it
  random: Delta Lake, Iceberg, SlateDB and FoundationDB use a random value, and
  Paimon uses a random ID per writer plus a sequence number. The systems with a
  content-derived identity are Git, Graft and ClickHouse's insert deduplication,
  and each of them treats equal content as one thing on purpose.

  **Systems (identity; where it is stored; what the writer does after an
  ambiguous result):**
  - **FoundationDB:** 16 random bytes; beside the data, expiring. A conflicting
  dummy commit, then a lookup of the ID. The feature is still marked
  experimental.
  - **Delta Lake:** a random UUID `txnId` in the commit file.
  `detectIdempotentSelfCommit` compares the winner's `txnId` with its own. That
  check is an internal option and is off by default.
  - **Iceberg:** a random UUID in each metadata file's name (snapshot IDs are
  random too). It checks whether its location is the current one or in
  `previousFiles`. This is the closest shape to `Tx.inLog`.
  - **Paimon:** a random UUID per writer plus a commit identifier, stored in
  each snapshot. It walks the snapshots looking for both.
  - **SlateDB:** a ULID drawn from its seeded `DbRand`, in the object's
  metadata. On `AlreadyExists` it reads the object's head and compares the ULID.
  - **Graft:** a content hash. An equal hash at the same position counts as
  success ("Someone (including us) pushed the same commit"). This is s4ql's
  mechanism.
  - **Kafka:** producer ID, epoch and sequence number, held by the broker and
  expiring after 7 days by default.
  - **DynamoDB:** a client token, kept for 10 minutes.
  - **CockroachDB:** a random transaction UUID, but the ambiguity goes to the
  client as `AmbiguousResultError` and is not resolved.
  - **Git:** a content hash with whole-second times. Two identical commits are
  one object, by intent.

  **Did anyone have this exact fault?** I found no report of the exact sequence.
  Graft has the same mechanism and treats it as benign, because for Graft a
  commit is a state, not an acknowledged event. ClickHouse has the fault class
  in plain form and documents it: separate inserts of the same rows are dropped
  as duplicates. Since 22.2 the fix is `insert_deduplication_token`, a token per
  insert.

  **What it means for the options:**
  - **(a) a random value in each commit's header:** this is what Delta, Iceberg,
  SlateDB and FoundationDB do. Their costs don't apply here: there is no
  separate ID store that has to expire and no reliance on object metadata. The
  value sits inside the immutable commit object that the log walk already reads.
  It works with deterministic simulation the way SlateDB does it: s4ql draws
  from `host.Uint64`, which is one seeded stream in the simulation and
  crypto/rand in production. It also leaves only one explanation for `ErrExists`
  on upload: the writer's own earlier attempt or a foreign object.
  - **(b) the instance ID in the header:** sound today, because the instance ID
  is already 128 random bits per process. But it only stays sound while an
  instance resolves an unknown outcome before it commits again. Paimon and Kafka
  add a sequence number for exactly that reason. It gains nothing over (a).
  - **(c) leave it and document it:** this leaves the lost update in place. A
  stopped or coarse clock can trigger it.
  - **Fourth option, a lease tenure (epoch) in the header:** it is (b) with a
  narrower scope. It would need a counter in the manifest that the lease doesn't
  have today, and it does nothing for this fault that (a) doesn't.
  - **What doesn't help:** a monotonic commit time in Delta's style, a sequence
  number taken from the parent, or comparing the existing object's checksum. Two
  writers on the same parent get equal values from all three.

  **Gaps:** Graft's plan for client provenance (`plans/grove.md`) is no longer
  on its main branch. Junio Hamano's git message and the etcd and Datomic claims
  rest on search excerpts, because the mail archives refused automated reads.
  Irmin, Noms, ZFS and WarpStream were not examined.

  **Commit:** `e1659103` on branch `research-commit-identity`, not pushed. It
  adds `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-ac8af594b4fc37c2
  b/docs/research/commit-identity.md` and its entry in `/Users/maragubot/Develop
  er/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-ac8af594b4fc37c2b/docs/research/README.md`.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agent "Research commit identity in other systems" finished · 9m 22s

⏺ The research supports (a). The note is on main:
  docs/research/commit-identity.md.

  What others do. Every system found that checks "did my own commit land?" after
  an ambiguous answer looks for a value that only that attempt carries, and
  nearly all make it random:

  - Delta Lake: a random UUID in each commit file, compared with the winner's.
  - Iceberg: a random UUID in each metadata file's name. The closest shape to
    what we do.
  - SlateDB: a random ID drawn from a seeded generator, so it stays
    deterministic in simulation, which is what we would do too.
  - FoundationDB: 16 random bytes per transaction, kept beside the data.
  - Paimon and Kafka: a writer ID plus a sequence number.

  Who identifies by content alone. Git, Graft, and ClickHouse's insert
  deduplication, and each treats equal content as one thing on purpose. Graft
  has exactly our mechanism and calls a match success ("someone, including us,
  pushed the same commit"), which is right for it because a commit there is a
  state, not an acknowledged event. ClickHouse had our kind of fault in plain
  form, separate inserts of the same rows dropped as duplicates, and fixed it
  with a token per insert.

  Our exact fault: no report of it found anywhere.

  For the options:
  - (a) is the common practice, and the costs others pay for it (a separate
    store of IDs that must expire) do not apply to us, because the value sits
    inside the commit object we already read.
  - (b) gains nothing over (a), and stays sound only while an instance resolves
    an unknown outcome before it commits again.
  - (c) leaves the lost update in place.

  Some sources could not be read directly and are marked so in the note.

  Do you want to settle it with (a)?

✻ Cogitated for 15s · done 2:10 PM
› Message from general-purpose
  Review of `core-checkpoints` (worktree reset to it, branch renamed to
  `core-checkpoints-review`). The worktree is clean and the scratch directory is
  deleted. I found no fault that a test run shows. The one fault in s4ql is on
  paper, at close after a stop. There are three gaps in the tests.

  **Findings, most important first**
  1. **Fault in s4ql (on paper, and worse than the todo file says): the
  checkpoint at close after a stop can delete the only durable copy of pages.**
  A passive checkpoint that a read holds back copies frames into the cache file
  and moves `nBackfill` forward without syncing the file. The sync only happens
  when `mxSafeFrame==mxFrame` (`internal/sqlite/c/sqlite3.c` ~69283-69292).
     - What happens at close after a stop: `DB.Close` closes the readers first
  and then the writer (`closeIdleLocked`, `core.go` ~838). The last connection
  takes EXCLUSIVE and runs a passive checkpoint from `nBackfill` with its own
  sync flags. On success it deletes the WAL and the shm (`sqlite3WalClose`,
  69482-69517). `sqlite3PagerClose` drops the result (63207), so `c.close()`
  never sees it.
     - Pages that an earlier held-back checkpoint copied are not written again
  by that retry. Suppose their writeback failed and was reported by the failed
  checkpoint's fsync (Linux reports it once per fd). Then the retry's fsync can
  succeed and the WAL is deleted, while the file on disk holds old pages. After
  an eviction or a power loss, the cache serves wrong rows under a position that
  says it is current.
     - A spare on the same cache directory that takes the lease without
  reopening continues from the same `nBackfill`, so it has the same exposure. A
  fresh open is safe, because WAL recovery sets `nBackfill=0` (68483).
     - The open item in `docs/todo/cache-checkpoints.md` names the WAL's pages.
  Under `full` those are synced at every commit, so the real exposure is pages
  of the cache file. It needs a sync failure, a held-back checkpoint and a
  crash, but it is silent. The remedy is still
  `SQLITE_DBCONFIG_NO_CKPT_ON_CLOSE` after a stop.
  2. **"A failed sync surfaces at the commit that caused it":**
     - It is true for the WAL. `walFrames` (~71151) syncs before it appends to
  the wal-index, and a failure fails the commit, so `TestDB_sync` is right.
     - It is not true for the cache file. That file is synced only by a
  checkpoint that copies the whole log, so the failure surfaces at a later
  commit's checkpoint. Writes from held-back checkpoints are never synced on
  their own.
     - The comment at `cache.go` ~270 ("so a sync that fails fails the commit
  that needed it") reads as covering both files. It is wrong for the cache file.
  3. **Coverage of the paths.** Every commit to the cache is checkpointed:
  `Tx.Commit` (`tx.go` 1434), `catchUp` (`catchup.go` 95), and `setUp`'s first
  commit, which is small and fails the open. `wal_autocheckpoint = 0` is set in
  `openConn`, which both bindings and every later reader (`read.go:55`) go
  through. The browser's SQLite is also 3.54.1 and knows `noop`.
     - Readers run SQLite's default sync, FULL, also when `syncOff` is set, so a
  reader that closes last syncs anyway. It does no harm.
  4. **A stop where nothing failed: I found none.**
     - Every BUSY variant (primary code 5) and every LOCKED variant (6) counts
  as busy.
     - The checkpoint steps with plain `Step`, so a cancelled context cannot
  give `SQLITE_INTERRUPT`. `StepContext` installs its progress handler only for
  its own step.
     - An empty or small WAL returns before any checkpoint. A spare or a
  read-only instance commits nothing, so it never checkpoints. A reader in the
  same process gives `held_back`, not an error.
     - The one real but harmless case: the simulation's `CloseCache` closes a
  connection that is in use, so a checkpoint would fail with MISUSE. That
  instance has crashed anyway.
     - Minor: a caller of the core who always commits from inside a scan never
  checkpoints, so the WAL grows without bound. The engine cannot do this,
  because it refuses a commit while rows are read (`engine/tx.go` 212).
  5. **Q3: `committed_and_stopped` returning nil is correct.** The spec says a
  commit is acknowledged once the swap succeeds, and the instance stops if the
  cache then fails. The nil passes unchanged through `engine/tx.go` 219 and
  `engine.go` 589, which builds the RETURNING rows before the commit, and
  through `tx.go` 91 in the root package. The next call gets `ErrStopped`.
  6. **Test gap:** a busy row (`after.busy`, another connection holds the
  checkpoint lock) is never tested. Planted fault F6 below survived.
  7. **Telemetry:** a busy event from an error sets `err` to nil
  (`checkpoint.go` ~68), so the event does not say whether it was BUSY or
  LOCKED. It is minor. I found no timing dependence or skips in the new tests.

  **Planted faults** (each run with the named `-run` set, and once with more
  filters added; each reverted afterwards)
  - F1, catch-up's checkpoint error dropped: **caught**. Caught by the catch-up
  subtest of `TestDB_checkpoint`.
    - My first version, which dropped the return value only, survived and is not
  a valid fault, because `d.stop` runs inside `checkpoint`.
  - F2, threshold never reached: **caught** by 7 subtests.
  - F3, `wal_autocheckpoint` turned off only on the writer: **caught**.
  - F4, `synchronous = normal`: **caught** by both `TestDB_sync` subtests.
  - F5, LOCKED treated as a failure: **caught** by the scan-loop subtest.
  - F6, a busy row treated as a failure: **survived**.
  - F7, held-back treated as a failure: **caught**.
  - F8, stop not made permanent: **caught** by 3 subtests.
  - F9, a failed `noop` treated as below the threshold: **caught**.
  - F10, `committed_and_stopped` not set in `Tx.Commit`: **caught**.
  - The simulation cell caught none of these on its own. The unit tests carry
  them.

  **Q5, cost**
  - These open instances with syncs on: the core's scenario tests (`w.serve` and
  `w.open` with `core.Options{}`), the engine's `TestDifferential`,
  `TestSimulation` and the other engine tests (`engine/simulation_test.go` 514,
  `differential_test.go` 634), and the root package's tests (`conn.go` 96).
  - None of the engine or root tests fails syncs, and their crashes keep the
  operating system's cache, so syncs off there would weaken no check. The `full`
  path stays covered by `TestDB_sync`, `TestDB_checkpoint`, the sync test at
  `core_test.go` 1331, `cache_test.go` 810 and 837, and the simulation's `syncs`
  cell.
  - The obstacle: `syncOff` can only be set from the core's own tests
  (`export_test.go` 63), so the engine and root tests would need a hook exported
  for tests.
  - In the core, `newWorld` could default to `SyncOff`, with `SyncOn`, which
  already exists, in the tests that fail syncs.

  **Not checked:** I did not repeat the builder's long runs or measure anything
  on Linux. I did not show finding 1 with a VFS that keeps volatile and durable
  state apart. I did not review the simulation cell's checks line by line, and
  did not run the browser build.

⏺ Agent "Review core checkpoints" finished · 16m 19s

⏺ The review of the cache's checkpoints is in, and a builder is closing its
  points and landing the branch. Nothing here needs you now; the commit-header
  question above is still the one open decision.

  What the review found:

  - No stop where nothing failed, and 9 of 10 planted faults caught.
  - One fault on paper, at close after a stop. When an instance has stopped for
    a failed sync, closing it still lets SQLite checkpoint once more and delete
    the WAL. That is the "try again in place" our spec rules out, and it can
    throw away the only good copy of some pages. The fix is to close without a
    checkpoint after a stop, so the WAL stays for the next start. Being built.
  - A precision about full. A failed sync surfaces at the commit that caused it
    for the WAL, but for the cache file only at a later checkpoint. Comments and
    notes are corrected to say so.
  - A cost I had not thought through. With full, every test that opens an
    instance now syncs at each commit, also the thousands of test instances that
    never test syncing. On Linux in CI that is real work for no value. The
    builder makes tests run with syncs off unless they test syncing, with a test
    per layer that proves production still runs full, and reports CI job times
    before and after.

  What will come to you later, once it is reproduced: a second instance sharing
  the cache directory could still reset the WAL after the first one stopped. The
  remedy would be to mark such a cache as suspect and rebuild it from the log
  on the next start, which the spec today says we do not do.

✻ Crunched for 46s · done 2:13 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I've finished the statement pass for upserts on branch `upsert-stmt`; the last
  commit is 5f334b03, not pushed. `make test` and `make lint` pass, and so did
  the 400-sequence `TestDifferential` (1150 s) and five minutes of fuzzing
  (`FuzzCheck` 3 min, `FuzzSplit` and `FuzzGenerate` 1 min each). The new tests
  also pass with `-count=20` and `-race`.

  **Typed form.** `stmt.Insert.Conflict` is a `stmt.Conflict` with fields `Span`
  (with `Present()`), `Target []Name` with offsets, `Action` (`DoNothing` or
  `DoUpdate`), `Set []Assignment` and `Where []Condition`. `excluded.c` is a new
  node kind, `ExprExcluded`, not a flag on `ExprColumn`, so old code can't
  misread it as a table column. It carries a new field `Expression.Table`, the
  qualifier as written, because SQLite prints it: `no such column: EXCLUDED.zz`.
  `HasColumn` is true for it, and `excluded.c is null` is the one null test
  that has an `Expression`. Parameters are numbered VALUES, then SET, then
  WHERE. SQLite counts `excluded.c` as 2 deep, and the depth edges are checked
  against it.

  **Refusals, each against what SQLite does** (all of these SQLite accepts
  unless noted):
  - No target, refused at DO. SQLite applies it to every uniqueness constraint;
  I refused it because the agreed form names the key.
  - Target with WHERE: SQLite matches the primary key anyway.
  - Target with ASC/DESC (SQLite ignores the order), NULLS FIRST (SQLite
  errors), COLLATE (SQLite matches by collation), parentheses, an expression or
  literal (matches nothing), TRUE, `t.k`, `excluded.k` (SQLite errors), or a
  column twice.
  - A second ON CONFLICT clause: SQLite uses the first one whose target matches.
  - An assignment to a target column: SQLite moves the row and gives UNIQUE if
  the new key is taken. The target is always the key in s4ql, so this refuses
  every key change in DO UPDATE.
  - A subquery in DO UPDATE SET or WHERE.
  - `t.v` in DO UPDATE, and `excluded.v` in an upsert into a table named
  `excluded`, where SQLite reads it as the table's own column (I measured 101
  where 105 would be the excluded reading).
  - `excluded` with a schema, or in single quotes, brackets or backticks.
  - Everything an update already refuses in SET and WHERE keeps its name.
  REPLACE and INSERT OR keep their old messages.
  - Syntax errors (`excluded.*`, `set excluded.v`, ON CONFLICT on an update)
  come through as SQLite gives them.
  - I did not limit DO UPDATE to 2,000 assignments, because SQLite counts only
  an update's set list.

  **Left to the engine step, because it needs the table:**
  - Whether the target is the primary key; otherwise SQLite gives `does not
  match any PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE constraint`.
  - Missing columns, in SQLite's order: SET is checked assignment by assignment,
  value before column.
  - Rows and counts, the DEFAULT behind an excluded column the list leaves out,
  exact types, NOT NULL, and query_only.

  **Outside `internal/sql/stmt`:**
  - `internal/engine/upsert.go` refuses an upsert as `ON CONFLICT clause` at its
  ON, called first in `textRefusal`, so before the database is read.
  - `engine.NewPlan` panics on an insert with a conflict clause, so nothing can
  plan one as a plain insert.
  - New tests in `internal/engine/upsert_test.go`.
  - An upsert rule in the engine's differential pair, placed before its
  length-limit rule, plus two `FuzzSQL` seeds.
  - The same rule, and a `TestPair` subtest, in
  `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck/drivercheck_test.go`.

  **Existing fault, found by a reviewer, not fixed:** on `main`, `update t1 set
  v1 = 1 where lower(v1) = 'a'` run through `callPrepareExec` fails the driver's
  differential pair. Its `checkRefused` rejects a refusal at prepare, and
  Prepare refuses text expressions there. The README Results table had also been
  stale since text functions; I updated it.

  **For Markus:** the list above of forms SQLite accepts and s4ql now refuses.
  `TestAllowList` now allows `ColumnRef.Table` for every statement; the error
  cases still guard `t.v`.

  Everything is recorded in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/a
  gent-a738805632c424ac0/docs/todo/upserts.md`, the "Upserts" section of
  `internal/sql/stmt/README.md`, and the diary at
  `docs/diary/2026-11-26-upsert-stmt.md`.

  One design-flavoured detail, for your information: SQLite accepts several
  forms that s4ql refuses here, because the agreed form names the key and we do
  not copy leniencies:

  - on conflict without a target (SQLite applies it to every uniqueness
    constraint);
  - a second on conflict clause;
  - do update changing a column of the key (SQLite moves the row);

  has no schedule: you start it by hand with inputs `since`, `pkgs`, `run`,
  `sample`, `seed`, `shards` and `issue`. AGENTS.md on `main` already says the
  same.
  - **Split and merge:** `-shard i/n` deals the mutants out in key order, with
  the ones in `equivalent.txt` first. So the shards are disjoint, together
  complete, and the same on every machine, and each gets its share of every file
  within one. Split by measured times would differ between runners, so I didn't
  use them. `-merge PATTERN` refuses a shard of other code (another
  fingerprint), a mutant with results in two shards, a result outside the
  selection, and an output folder that already has results. It drops a last line
  that a killed shard cut short.
  - **What a round covers:** every declaration of `-pkgs all` that changed since
  the last recorded round. That is the commit in
  `internal/sql/mutation/round.txt`, which a builder writes after working
  through the round's issue. Or it is a later whole default round on `main` that
  left no work: that run uploads an artifact `mutation-round`, and the next run
  finds it through the API and checks it sits between `round.txt` and its own
  commit. `round.txt` starts at 8c53a53f (#156), so the next round is 2,208
  mutants: several statements in one `Exec` (#157–#168) and #172.
  - **Shards and concurrency:** GitHub's limits page gives 60 concurrent jobs on
  Team, and `gh api` shows maragudk is on Team. A public repo's `ubuntu-latest`
  runner has 4 CPUs, 16 GB and 14 GB of disk. One shard per 150 mutants, at
  most 6 by default, which leaves room for a pull request's CI (29 jobs) and the
  nightly (15). Each shard runs 3 mutants at once with 3 GiB of memory per test
  binary.
  - **Timeout rule:** each shard plans on its own runner, timing every stage
  alone. A stage's limit is 20 times its baseline, at least 1 minute. Timeouts
  count as killed and are listed separately by `file:line` so they can be rerun.
  - **Issue:** one open issue labelled `mutation`. Each run replaces its body
  with the latest report and adds a comment. The title names the commit the
  round counts from. A cancelled run, or one with nothing changed, opens
  nothing.
  - **Sample and cost:** `-sample f -seed s` draws a seeded fraction. The
  summary scales the unexplained survivors up in proportion; with none found
  among n mutants it gives an upper bound of 3/n at 95% (the rule of three). It
  says it cannot clear mutants outside the sample. Every summary shows
  runner-minutes, mutants per runner-minute, survivors found, setup and planning
  time per shard, and where the workers' time went (by layer, and the
  survivors' share).

  ⎿  Resuming agent a2e9abc

⏺ The mutation workflow is merged (#176, 29 checks passed). It has no schedule:
  it is started by hand, splits the mutants over at most 6 runners by default,
  and every run reports its cost in runner-minutes beside what it found. A
  sampled run gives a figure for the whole and says plainly that it cannot clear
  the mutants it did not draw.

  Its builder is now doing two runs: a tiny one to prove the workflow end to
  end, then a one-in-five sample of what no round has covered yet (several
  statements and the new text code, 2,208 mutants in all). The full round does
  not start unless the sample shows it is warranted; I decide that from its
  numbers.

✻ Cooked for 40s · done 2:26 PM

❯ a), let's add the random value to the commit object (not a UUID though)       

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Recorded and pushed: every commit object gets a random value in its header,
  and not a UUID.

  How I take "not a UUID", as a detail: 16 plain random bytes from the host's
  randomness, with no version bits and no text form. It is drawn once per
  transaction, so a retried upload sends the same bytes and still finds its own
  object. In the simulation it comes from the seeded host, so runs stay
  deterministic. The spec's rule that a commit object is named by the checksum
  of its content is unchanged.

  I build it right after the annotation change lands, since both change the same
  format file and would collide. The failing test for the fault is waiting on a
  branch and becomes the proof.

  Nothing else is waiting on you.
